i_{D}\colon\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})^{D}}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})^{D}}
are
C^{\infty}
. We show the former, since the latter is similar. Let
A_{1}=(\gamma_{1},\varepsilon_{1})
and
A_{2}=(\gamma_{2},\varepsilon_{2})
be elements of
\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})^{D}}
and set
A_{3}=A_{1}A_{2}=(\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2},\varepsilon_{3})
. We choose a
\delta_{3}>0
such that
V(A_{3};\delta_{3})
is an open neighborhood of
A_{3}
(due to Lemma
A.2
) and that (
A.9
) holds for
\gamma=\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}
and
\delta_{1}=\delta_{3}
. The multiplication map
m\colon\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
is
C^{\infty}
. Therefore, there exist open sets
U(\gamma_{1};\delta_{1})
and
U(\gamma_{2};\delta_{2})
such that
m(U(\gamma_{1};\delta_{1})\times U(\gamma_{2};\delta_{2}))\subseteq U(\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2};\delta_{3})
, that
U(\gamma_{j};\delta_{j})
is evenly covered by
p_{D}
(see
[
32
, p. 278]
), and that (
A.9
) holds for
U(\gamma_{j};\delta_{j})
,
j=1,2
. It follows that
V(A_{j};\delta_{j})
is an open neighborhood of
A_{j}
for
j=1,2
. If we can prove for sufficiently small
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
,

(A.10)

m_{D}(V(A_{1};\delta_{1})\times V(A_{2};\delta_{2}))\subseteq V(A_{3};\delta_{3})

(which is nontrivial if one looks merely at the definition of
V(A_{j};\delta_{j})
), then by the construction of the
C^{\infty}
manifold structure on
\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})^{D}}
, we can conclude that
m_{D}
is
C^{\infty}
on
V(A_{1};\delta_{1})\times V(A_{2};\delta_{2})
, and hence on the whole
\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})^{D}}\times\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})^{D}}
by the arbitrariness of
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
.

To find
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
such that (
A.10
) holds, we return to the definition (
A.1
). By construction,
V(A_{j};\delta_{j})=U(A_{j};\delta_{j},r)
for
j=1,2
(we have chosen a common
r>0
) and
V(A_{3};\delta_{3})=U(A_{3};\delta_{3},r_{3})
. Let
(A_{1}^{\prime},A_{2}^{\prime})=((\gamma_{1}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{1}^{\prime}),(\gamma_{2}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{2}^{\prime}))\in V(A_{1};\delta_{1})\times V(A_{2};\delta_{2})=U(A_{1};\delta_{1},r)\times U(A_{2};\delta_{2},r)
. By (
2.2
), we have

\displaystyle A_{1}^{\prime}A_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}\gamma_{2}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{1}^{\prime}\varepsilon_{2}^{\prime}\phi_{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}}(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}\tau)\phi_{\gamma_{2}^{\prime}}(\tau)),

\displaystyle A_{3}=A_{1}A_{2}

\displaystyle=(\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2},\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}\phi_{\gamma_{1}}(\gamma_{2}\tau)\phi_{\gamma_{2}}(\tau)),

where
\phi_{\gamma}(\tau)=(c^{\prime}\tau+d^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{D}}
and
\gamma\tau=\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}
if
\gamma=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Note that

(A.11)

\lVert\varepsilon_{1}^{\prime}\varepsilon_{2}^{\prime}\phi_{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}}(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}\tau)\phi_{\gamma_{2}^{\prime}}(\tau)-\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}\phi_{\gamma_{1}}(\gamma_{2}\tau)\phi_{\gamma_{2}}(\tau)\rVert\leq\lVert\varepsilon_{1}^{\prime}\phi_{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}}(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}\tau)\rVert\cdot\lVert\varepsilon_{2}^{\prime}\phi_{\gamma_{2}^{\prime}}(\tau)-\varepsilon_{2}\phi_{\gamma_{2}}(\tau)\rVert\\
+\lVert\varepsilon_{2}\phi_{\gamma_{2}}(\tau)\rVert\cdot\left(\lVert\varepsilon_{1}^{\prime}\phi_{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}}(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}\tau)-\varepsilon_{1}^{\prime}\phi_{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}}(\gamma_{2}\tau)\rVert+\lVert\varepsilon_{1}^{\prime}\phi_{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}}(\gamma_{2}\tau)-\varepsilon_{1}\phi_{\gamma_{1}}(\gamma_{2}\tau)\rVert\right).

Since
A_{2}^{\prime}\in V(A_{2};\delta_{2})=U(A_{2};\delta_{2},r)
we have
\lVert\varepsilon_{2}^{\prime}\phi_{\gamma_{2}^{\prime}}(\tau)-\varepsilon_{2}\phi_{\gamma_{2}}(\tau)\rVert<r
, and
\lVert\gamma_{2}^{\prime}-\gamma_{2}\rVert<\delta_{2}
. Since
\gamma_{2}\overline{B}
is a compact set in
\mathfrak{H}
, shrinking
\delta_{1}
if necessary we obtain
\lVert\varepsilon_{1}^{\prime}\phi_{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}}(\gamma_{2}\tau)-\varepsilon_{1}\phi_{\gamma_{1}}(\gamma_{2}\tau)\rVert<r
. By Lagrange’s Mean Value Theorem, we have
\lVert\varepsilon_{1}^{\prime}\phi_{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}}(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}\tau)-\varepsilon_{1}^{\prime}\phi_{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}}(\gamma_{2}\tau)\rVert<r
if
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
are sufficiently small. Inserting these estimates into (
A.11
) we find that

\lVert\varepsilon_{1}^{\prime}\varepsilon_{2}^{\prime}\phi_{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}}(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}\tau)\phi_{\gamma_{2}^{\prime}}(\tau)-\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}\phi_{\gamma_{1}}(\gamma_{2}\tau)\phi_{\gamma_{2}}(\tau)\rVert<r\cdot\lVert\varepsilon_{1}^{\prime}\phi_{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}}(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}\tau)\rVert+2r\cdot\lVert\varepsilon_{2}\phi_{\gamma_{2}}(\tau)\rVert

for sufficiently small
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
and for
(A_{1}^{\prime},A_{2}^{\prime})\in V(A_{1};\delta_{1})\times V(A_{2};\delta_{2})=U(A_{1};\delta_{1},r)\times U(A_{2};\delta_{2},r)
. The function
(\gamma_{1}^{\prime},\gamma_{2}^{\prime},\tau)\mapsto\phi_{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}}(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}\tau)
is bounded since
(\gamma_{1}^{\prime},\gamma_{2}^{\prime},\tau)
lies in a compact set in
\mathbb{R}^{4}\times\mathbb{R}^{4}\times\overline{B}
. Therefore,

\lVert\varepsilon_{1}^{\prime}\varepsilon_{2}^{\prime}\phi_{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}}(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}\tau)\phi_{\gamma_{2}^{\prime}}(\tau)-\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}\phi_{\gamma_{1}}(\gamma_{2}\tau)\phi_{\gamma_{2}}(\tau)\rVert<rC,

where
C
is a positive constant independent of
(A_{1}^{\prime},A_{2}^{\prime})
. By Remark
A.3
(2), we can shrink
r
so that
rC<r_{3}
. It follows from this,
m(U(\gamma_{1};\delta_{1})\times U(\gamma_{2};\delta_{2}))\subseteq U(\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2};\delta_{3})
, and
V(A_{3};\delta_{3})=U(A_{3};\delta_{3},r_{3})
that
m_{D}(A_{1}^{\prime},A_{2}^{\prime})=A_{1}^{\prime}A_{2}^{\prime}\in V(A_{3};\delta_{3})
. Now (
A.10
) follows since
A_{1}^{\prime}
and
A_{2}^{\prime}
are arbitrary.

Finally, the assertion that
p_{D}
is a Lie group homomorphism is merely a combination of what has been proved above.
∎

To conclude this appendix, we mention two more observations about
D
-covers. The first is an application in which Zhu
[
58
]
constructed unitary characters and
\mathbb{Z}
-valued homomorphisms of
\Gamma_{0}(N)
. The
D
-cover formalism plays a key role in this construction; cf.
[
58
, Propositions 2.1 and 3.1]
.

The second observation addresses an essential difference between the double cover of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and the
D
-covers when
\gcd(D,6)=1
. This is a new result. To begin with, we introduce some terminology.

Definition A.5
.

Let
D
be a positive integer and let
G
be a subgroup of
\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})^{D}}
. Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. We say
G
is
n
-fold
if for every
(\gamma_{0},\varepsilon_{0})\in G
, we have

\#\{(\gamma,\varepsilon)\in G\colon\gamma=\gamma_{0}\}=n.

Let
H
be a subgroup of
G
. If
p_{D}(H)=p_{D}(G)
(see Proposition
A.4
(c) for
p_{D}
), then we say
H
and
G
are
base-equivalent
.

Proposition A.6
.

Let
D
be a positive integer and let
G
be a subgroup of
\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})^{D}}
. Then there exists a unique
n>0
such that
G
is
n
-fold. Moreover, we have
n\mid D
.

Proof.

The uniqueness of
n
is immediate. To show the existence, let
R=\{(I,\varepsilon)\in G\}
, and
n=\#R
. We now prove
G
is
n
-fold. Equivalently, we need to prove that for every
\gamma_{0}\in p_{D}(G)
, we have
\#\{(\gamma_{0},\varepsilon)\in G\}=n
. Let
(\gamma_{0},\varepsilon)\in G
; then
(\gamma_{0},\varepsilon)\cdot g=(\gamma_{0},\varepsilon^{\prime})\in G
for all
g\in R
, and hence
\#\{(\gamma_{0},\varepsilon)\in G\}\geq n
. On the other hand, if
(\gamma_{0},\varepsilon_{1}),(\gamma_{0},\varepsilon_{2})\in G
, then
(\gamma_{0},\varepsilon_{1})^{-1}(\gamma_{0},\varepsilon_{2})\in R
, and hence
\#\{(\gamma_{0},\varepsilon)\in G\}\leq\#((\gamma_{0},\varepsilon_{1})\cdot R)=n
. Therefore,
\#\{(\gamma_{0},\varepsilon)\in G\}=n
. Finally, since
R
is a subgroup of
\{(I,\varepsilon)\colon\varepsilon^{D}=1\}
, we have
n\mid D
.
∎

If
G
is a subgroup of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
, then a
1
-fold base-equivalent subgroup of
\widetilde{G^{D}}
is canonically isomorphic to
G
. Namely,
G
can be realized as a subgroup of
\widetilde{G^{D}}
. Geometrically speaking, a
1
-fold base-equivalent subgroup of
\widetilde{G^{D}}
is a section of the covering map
p_{D}\colon\widetilde{G^{D}}\rightarrow G
that is also a subgroup. The following proposition determines for which
D
the group
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{D}}
has a
1
-fold base-equivalent subgroup.

Proposition A.7
.

Let
D=2^{\alpha}3^{\beta}D_{0}
where
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
D_{0}\geq 1
satisfies
\gcd(D_{0},6)=1
. Then
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{D}}
has a
2^{\alpha}3^{\beta}
-fold base-equivalent subgroup. In particular, if
\gcd(D,6)=1
, then
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{D}}
has a subgroup canonically isomorphic to
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

The proof rests on the following general lemma.

Lemma A.8
.

Let
D\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
,
k=\frac{t}{D}
where
t\in\mathbb{Z}
is coprime to
D
. Let
G
be a subgroup of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. If there exists a linear character
\chi\colon\widetilde{G^{D}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
satisfying

•

for all
\gamma\in\widetilde{G^{D}}
we have
\chi(\gamma)^{D}=1
,

•

\chi\left(I,\mathfrak{e}\left(1/D\right)\right)=\mathfrak{e}\left(-k\right)
,

then
\widetilde{G^{D}}
has a
1
-fold base-equivalent subgroup.

Proof.

Let
R
be a set of generators of
G
(e.g.,
R=G
). For any
\gamma\in R
, let
m_{\gamma}\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfy
\chi(\widetilde{\gamma})\mathfrak{e}\left(-km_{\gamma}\right)=1
. Such an
m_{\gamma}
exists due to the first assumption on
\chi
and
\gcd(D,t)=1
. Set

R^{\prime}=\{\widetilde{\gamma}\cdot(I,\mathfrak{e}\left(m_{\gamma}/D\right))\colon\gamma\in R\}.

Then for all
g\in R^{\prime}
, we have

(A.12)

\chi(g)=\chi(\widetilde{\gamma})\chi(I,\mathfrak{e}\left(m_{\gamma}/D\right))=\chi(\widetilde{\gamma})\mathfrak{e}\left(-km_{\gamma}\right)=1,

where we have used the second assumption on
\chi
in the second equality. Let
H
be the subgroup of
\widetilde{G^{D}}
generated by
R^{\prime}
. We claim that
H
is a
1
-fold base-equivalent subgroup. The groups
H
and
\widetilde{G^{D}}
are obviously base-equivalent. To see
H
is
1
-fold, let
(\gamma,\varepsilon_{1})
and
(\gamma,\varepsilon_{2})
be arbitrary elements of
H
whose first components are the same. Then they are both products of some
g
or
g^{-1}
where
g\in R^{\prime}
, so by (
A.12
)
\chi(\gamma,\varepsilon_{1})=\chi(\gamma,\varepsilon_{2})=1
. It follows that

1=\chi(\gamma,\varepsilon_{2})=\chi(\gamma,\varepsilon_{1})\chi(I,\varepsilon_{2}\varepsilon_{1}^{-1})=\chi(I,\varepsilon_{2}\varepsilon_{1}^{-1}).

Since
\chi(I,\mathfrak{e}\left(j/D\right))=1
if and only if
D\mid j
(by the second assumption on
\chi
and
\gcd(D,t)=1
), we have
\varepsilon_{2}\varepsilon_{1}^{-1}=1
, namely,
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{2}
.
∎

Remark A.9
.

The proof actually constructs a generating set of a
1
-fold base-equivalent subgroup, namely
R^{\prime}=\{(\gamma,\mathfrak{e}\left(m_{\gamma}/D\right))\colon\gamma\in R\}
, where
R
is an arbitrary generating set of
G
. To find
m_{\gamma}
occurring therein, we should first find an integer
t
and a character
\chi
subject to the conditions stated in this lemma, and then find an
m_{\gamma}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\chi(\widetilde{\gamma})=\mathfrak{e}\left(tm_{\gamma}/D\right)
. For
G=\Gamma_{0}(N)
, this can usually (not always) be done by using the character of some eta-quotient of cover index
D
.

To prove Proposition
A.7
, we need to know the group of linear characters of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{D}}
. Recall that
T=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{1}&{1}\\
{0}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
S=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{0}&{-1}\\
{1}&{0}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, and
I=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{1}&{0}\\
{0}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

Lemma A.10
.

[
57
, Lemma 5.2]

The group
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{D}}
has a presentation with generators
\widetilde{T}
,
\widetilde{S}
and relations
\widetilde{S}^{4D}=\widetilde{I}
,
(\widetilde{S}\widetilde{T})^{3}=\widetilde{S}^{2}
,
\widetilde{S}^{4}\widetilde{T}=\widetilde{T}\widetilde{S}^{4}
. As a consequence, there are exactly
12D
linear characters on
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{D}}
which form a cyclic group.

Proof.

See
[
57
, Lemma 5.2]
.
∎

The linear characters of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{D}}
are exactly the followings:

\widetilde{T}\mapsto\mathfrak{e}\left(\frac{i}{12D}\right),\qquad\widetilde{S}\mapsto\mathfrak{e}\left(-\frac{i}{4D}\right),

where
i
ranges over
1,2,\dots,12D
.

Proof of Proposition
A.7
.

First assume that
\gcd(D,6)=1
. We need to prove
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{D}}
has a
1
-fold base-equivalent subgroup. Let
t=12
,
k=\frac{12}{D}
, and let
\chi\colon\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{D}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
be the character given by
\chi(\widetilde{T})=\mathfrak{e}\left(1/D\right)
and
\chi(\widetilde{S})=\mathfrak{e}\left(-3/D\right)
. Then all the conditions of Lemma
A.8
(with
G=\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
) are fulfilled. (To see why
\chi(I,\mathfrak{e}\left(1/D\right))=\mathfrak{e}\left(-k\right)
, we use the fact that
\widetilde{S}^{4}=(I,\mathfrak{e}\left(1/D\right))
.) Therefore, Lemma
A.8
implies that
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{D}}
has a
1
-fold base-equivalent subgroup.

Then assume that
D
is general. Let
p_{D,D_{0}}\colon\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{D}}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{D_{0}}}
be the projection introduced in Remark
2.2
. Then
p_{D,D_{0}}
is a surjective group homomorphism, and
\ker p_{D,D_{0}}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2^{\alpha}3^{\beta}\mathbb{Z}
. Since
\gcd(D_{0},6)=1
, there is a
1
-fold base-equivalent subgroup
H
of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{D_{0}}}
. Then
p_{D,D_{0}}^{-1}(H)
is a
2^{\alpha}3^{\beta}
-fold base-equivalent subgroup of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{D}}
.
∎

Actually, a
2^{\alpha}3^{\beta}
-fold base-equivalent subgroup of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{D}}
has been explicitly constructed in the above proof. For example, suppose that
\gcd(D,6)=1
. Let
\chi
be the character used in the above proof. Let
m_{T},m_{S}
be integers satisfying
12m_{T}\equiv 1\bmod{D}
and
4m_{S}\equiv-1\bmod{D}
, then we have

\chi(\widetilde{T})=\mathfrak{e}\left(1/D\right)=\mathfrak{e}\left(12m_{T}/D\right),\qquad\chi(\widetilde{S})=\mathfrak{e}\left(-3/D\right)=\mathfrak{e}\left(12m_{S}/D\right).

It then follows from Remark
A.9
that the group
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{\bullet}
generated by
(T,\mathfrak{e}\left(m_{T}/D\right))
and
(S,\mathfrak{e}\left(m_{S}/D\right))
is a
1
-fold base-equivalent subgroup of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{D}}
. In other words,
p_{D}
restricts to a group isomorphism from
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{\bullet}
onto
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Indeed, there is a more explicit construction:

Theorem A.11
.

Let
D
be a positive integer with
\gcd(D,6)=1
. Let
m\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfy
12m\equiv 1\bmod{D}
. Then

\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{\bullet}:=\left\{\left(\gamma,\mathfrak{e}\left(m\Psi(\gamma)/D\right)\right)\colon\gamma\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\right\}

is a
1
-fold base-equivalent subgroup of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{D}}
, where
\Psi
is defined in (
2.7
).

Proof.

Setting in (
2.8
)
N=1
and
r_{1}=24/D
we obtain a character
\chi_{1}\colon\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{D}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
,
(\gamma,\varepsilon)\mapsto\varepsilon^{-12}\mathfrak{e}\left(\frac{1}{D}\Psi(\gamma)\right)
. Then
\chi=\chi_{1}
and
t=12
satisfy the conditions of Lemma
A.8
(with
G=\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
). Therefore, by Remark
A.9
(with
R=\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
),
R^{\prime}=\{(\gamma,\mathfrak{e}\left(m_{\gamma}/D\right))\colon\gamma\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\}
generates a
1
-fold base-equivalent subgroup of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{D}}
, where
m_{\gamma}
should satisfy
\chi_{1}(\widetilde{\gamma})=\mathfrak{e}\left(12m_{\gamma}/D\right)
. Indeed,
m_{\gamma}=m\Psi(\gamma)
suffices as
12m\equiv 1\bmod{D}
. Moreover, it is immediate that the group generated by
R^{\prime}
is
R^{\prime}
itself since it is
1
-fold.
∎

Remark A.12
.

In fact, the character
\chi_{1}
above is the same as the character
\chi
constructed in the proof of Proposition
A.7
.

Finally, we show by an example that when
\gcd(D,6)>1
,
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{D}}
may have no
1
-fold base-equivalent subgroup:

Proposition A.13
.

Let
\alpha
be a positive integer. Then the only base-equivalent subgroup of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{2^{\alpha}}}
is this group itself.

Proof.

Let
G
be an arbitrary base-equivalent subgroup of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{2^{\alpha}}}
. Then there is a
t\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
(S,\mathfrak{e}\left(t/2^{\alpha}\right))\in G
. Then
(I,\mathfrak{e}\left((4t+1)/2^{\alpha}\right))=(S,\mathfrak{e}\left(t/2^{\alpha}\right))^{4}\in G
. Let
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfy
a(4t+1)+b2^{\alpha}=1
. Then
(I,\mathfrak{e}\left(1/2^{\alpha}\right))=(I,\mathfrak{e}\left((a(4t+1)+b2^{\alpha})/2^{\alpha}\right))=(I,\mathfrak{e}\left((4t+1)/2^{\alpha}\right))^{a}\in G
. It follows that
\widetilde{S}=(S,\mathfrak{e}\left(t/2^{\alpha}\right))\cdot(I,\mathfrak{e}\left(1/2^{\alpha}\right))^{-t}\in G
. Similarly,
\widetilde{T}\in G
. By Lemma
A.10
,
\widetilde{T}
and
\widetilde{S}
generate
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{2^{\alpha}}}
, and hence
G=\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})^{2^{\alpha}}}
.
∎

Appendix B
Usage of SageMath code

The SageMath code for computing eta-quotients,
D
-covers, dimensions, and other related quantities, as well as for verifying the theorems and generating the tables can be found at
http://xjzmath.com/code/SageCodeDimensionRationalWeight.html
.
